# ExpBenchmark on an AMD EPYC 7763 GitHub runner, 20 November 2026: exp, run twice at a5683c3
#
# Produced by .github/workflows/benchmark.yml, dispatched twice by bench/dispatch-bench.sh --host
# epyc7763 on main at a5683c3, each of (Exp|Expm1|Log|Log10|Log1p)Benchmark: run 44710193581 (20
# November 2026, 09:23 UTC) and its re-run 44719173929 (10:26 UTC). They are the two EPYC 7763 runs
# that issue #30 asks for. The EPYC 7763 files of exp, expm1, log and log1p of 2 and 4 November are
# older and stay; log10 had none. This file holds ExpBenchmark's blocks and rows of both runs: the
# first run's header, table and raw output, then, after the line "# Run 2 of 2 of this file", the
# second's. bench/record.py write made it from the two artifacts, and bench/record.py check
# bench/results/exp-epyc7763-2026-11-20.txt --run 44710193581 --run 44719173929 passes. The lines
# from "Host" to "Command" of each run are the workflow's header; "Array" describes the benchmark;
# the min/avg/max tables are taken from JMH's "Result" blocks in the raw output.
#
# The code: Exp.java has changed since a5683c3, and no run has timed it since: this file times
# a5683c3's exp, not main's at 7d7188f. The change is a fault constant (DROP_K_FORCING, read once
# into a static final, which folds away with no fault planted), the k conversion's argument built in
# a local before its blend, in both of its bodies, and Javadoc. Faults.java and VFdlibm.java changed
# in Javadoc only. ExpBenchmark.java is unchanged. The benchmark workflow has since added -foe true
# to the JMH options, which these two runs lack (their JMH lines): without it JMH prints <failure>
# for a row that fails and still ends the run green. Every row of ExpBenchmark has a Result block in
# both runs, which bench/record.py requires, so no row of this file is missing.
#
# The run held 5 classes; this file has ExpBenchmark's blocks and rows of it.
#
# Host:        epyc7763 (expected CPU 'AMD EPYC 7763 64-Core Processor')
# Commit:      a5683c3cbb3c2906fc466bdfc0cbd69890de32e4 (main)

# Command:     mvn -B -Pjmh test-compile exec:exec -Djmh.args="<the above>"

#              replaces one input in 16 with a rare one (a NaN, an overflow, an underflow, a
#              subnormal result)
#
# Reading it (AVX2: UseAVX=2, MaxVectorSize=32, preferred species 256 bits; datapath 256:128 = 2.00,
# 512:256 = 1.00; each figure from the tables below, run 1 / run 2 where two are given):
# - The two runs agree: every 256-bit, preferred, Max, and scalar row's minimum in run 2 is 0.97 to
#   1.05 of run 1's (18 rows). Each run is its own runner VM; run 1 started at load average 2.37 and
#   run 2 at 1.07. Below, two figures are run 1 / run 2.
# - Minimums, ns per 1024 doubles, at 256 bits. Common data: blended 5,581 / 5,424, finish 2,749 /
#   2,727, scalar StrictMath 11,086 / 11,090. Mixed data: blended 6,429 / 6,369, finish 4,292 /
#   4,256, scalar StrictMath 11,749 / 11,739. The preferred rows read as the 256-bit ones, as the
#   species is 256 bits here.
# - Blended against finish: 1.99 to 2.13 times on common data and 1.50 to 1.57 on mixed, over the
#   256-bit and preferred rows of both runs, so every row is over 1.3x apart and in the same
#   direction in both runs, finish having the lowest minimum. Finish is 4.0 / 4.1 times faster than
#   the scalar loop on common data and 2.7 / 2.8 on mixed (256 bits). This reads a direction for
#   exp's two arms on this host; which one VFdlibm.exp keeps is not decided here, it waits for the
#   AVX-512 hosts (issue #30).
# - The 128-bit and 512-bit rows read 99,241 to 279,666 ns, 24 to 68 times the same arm's 256-bit
#   row of the same input, and allocate 528,384 to 1,349,633 B/op in JMH's table: they do not time
#   vector code. The 512-bit rows are wider than this host's vectors (Vector: MaxVectorSize=32
#   above), and a 128-bit species has two lanes, where x86 C2 lowers no mask (SKILLS.md). Do not
#   quote them as the code's speed.
# - At SPECIES_MAX the finish arm boxes on mixed data: finishMax mixed reads 10,276 / 10,556 ns
#   (36,992 B/op) against finish256 mixed 4,292 / 4,256 ns (0.015 B/op), 2.39 / 2.48 times; on
#   common data finishMax reads 2,607 / 2,748 ns, against finish256's 2,749 / 2,727. The other Max
#   rows are within 6% of their 256-bit rows.
# - The finishPairBoxed rows, which box (an experiment for code generators), read 14,661 / 14,591 ns
#   on common data at 256 bits and 14,891 / 14,848 on mixed, 5.33 / 5.35 and 3.47 / 3.49 times
#   finish256, and slower than the scalar loop (11,086 / 11,090 and 11,749 / 11,739); they allocate
#   59,392 and 61,632 B/op.
# - Allocation (gc.alloc.rate.norm): every 256-bit, preferred and Max row reads 0.023 B/op or less
#   in both runs (finishMax on mixed data apart), and the scalar rows 0.041 or less.
